Decidability for the theory of modules over a Prüfer domain
Lorna Gregory

Abstract
. In this paper we give elementary conditions completely characterising when the theory of modules of a Prüfer domain is decidable. Using these results, we show that the theory of modules of the ring of integer valued polynomials is decidable.



1.
Introduction

In this article, we give a complete characterization of when the theory of modules of a recursive Prüfer domain is decidable.

Prüfer domains are a much studied class of rings, including many classically important rings and classes of rings. For example, they include Dedekind domains and hence rings of integers of number fields; Bézout domains and hence the ring of complex entire functions
[
Hel40
, Thm. 9]
and the ring of algebraic integers
[
Kap74
, Thm. 102]
; the ring of integer valued polynomials with rational coefficients
[
CC97
, VI.1.7]
and the real holomorphy ring of a formally real field
[
Bec82
, 2.16]
.

Prüfer domains have provided a rich supply of rings for which the decidability of modules can be determined. The theory of modules of a ring
R
is said to be decidable if there is an algorithm which decides whether a given first order sentence in the language of
R
-modules is true in all
R
-modules.

The first non-trivial example of a ring with decidable theory of modules was given by Szmielew,
[
Szm55
]
, who showed that the theory of abelian groups (or equivalently
\mathbb{Z}
-modules) is decidable. This result was generalised by Eklof and Fischer,
[
EF72
]
, to certain Dedekind domains, among them certain rings of integers, and they showed that, for a (recursive) field
k
with a splitting algorithm, the theory of
k[x]
-modules is decidable.

The most recent effort to understand decidability of theories of modules over Prüfer domains started with a paper,
[
PPT07
]
, of Puninski, Puninskaya and Toffalori. They showed that a recursive valuation domain with dense archimedean value group has decidable theory of modules if and only if its set of units is recursive. Proving a conjecture in
[
PPT07
]
, we show in
[
Gre15
]
that an arbitrary recursive valuation domain has decidable theory of modules if and only if the radical relation
a\in\textnormal{rad}bR
is recursive.

The theory of modules of Bézout domains of the form
D+XQ[X]\subseteq Q[X]
, where
D
is a principal ideal domain with field of fractions
Q
, is shown in
[
PT14
]
to be decidable under certain reasonable effective conditions on
D
. In particular, it is shown that
\mathbb{Z}+X\mathbb{Q}[X]
has decidable theory of modules. The theory of modules of the ring of algebraic integers, along with some other Bézout domains with Krull dimension
1
, is shown to have decidable theory of modules in
[
LTP17
]
.

Work towards characterising when a general Prüfer domain has decidable theory of modules was started in the articles
[
GLPT18
]
and
[
GLT19
]
, and is finished in the present one.
We will describe the results of these articles whilst describing the main result of this article.

First a reminder of the setup for proving decidability results for theories of modules.
Thanks to the Baur-Monk theorem, if
R
is a recursive ring then the theory of
R
-modules is decidable if and only if there exists an algorithm which, given
\nicefrac{{\varphi_{1}}}{{\psi_{1}}},\ldots,\nicefrac{{\varphi_{l}}}{{\psi_{l}}}
pairs of pp-formulae and intervals
[n_{1},m_{1}],\ldots,[n_{l},m_{l}]\subseteq\mathbb{N}
where
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, answers whether there exists an
R
-module
M
such that, for all
1\leq i\leq l
,
\left|\varphi(M)/\psi(M)\right|\in[n_{i},m_{i}]
. The existence of an algorithm answering this question when
[n_{i},m_{i}]
are either
[1,1]
or
[1,\infty]
is equivalent to the existence of an algorithm deciding whether one Ziegler basic open set is contained in a finite union of other Ziegler basic open sets
(for the definition of the Ziegler spectrum see
2.1
).

We characterise when the theory of modules of a Prüfer domain
R
is decidable in terms of the recursivity of three sets:

\mathrm{DPR}(R)
,
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and
X(R)
. Each of these sets is a subset of
R^{n}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
for some
n,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. For the sets
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and
X(R)
, we postpone their definitions to section
3
and in this introduction we instead give some indication of their meaning.

For any commutative ring
R
, the set
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is defined as the set of
(a,b,c,d)\in R^{4}
such that for all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\lhd R
with
\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q}\neq R
, either
a\in\mathfrak{p}
,
b\notin\mathfrak{p}
,
c\in\mathfrak{q}
or
d\notin\mathfrak{q}
.
This set was introduced in
[
GLPT18
]
as a generalisation of the radical relation
a\in\textnormal{rad}bR
.
For a recursive Bézout domain it is shown there that
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive if and only if there is an algorithm deciding inclusions of Ziegler basic open sets. For recursive Prüfer domains, analogous sufficient conditions were given for there to exist an algorithm deciding inclusions of Ziegler basic open sets. Building heavily on those results, we extend the equivalence given in
[
GLPT18
]
for Bézout domains to all recursive Prüfer domains (see
3.8
). As a consequence we get the following theorem.

Theorem.
(See
3.9
)

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain such that
R/\mathfrak{m}
is infinite for all maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
. The theory of
R
-modules is decidable if and only if
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive.

For rings
R
with a pair of pp-formulae
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}
and an
R
-module
M
such that
\left|\varphi(M)/\psi(M)\right|
is finite but not equal to
1
, in particular for commutative rings with finite non-zero modules, we need to do more than show that there is an algorithm deciding inclusions of Ziegler basic open sets.

For any ring
R
, if the theory of
R
-modules is decidable then the theory of modules of size
n
is decidable uniformly in
n
.
In
3.19
, we introduce a set
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
, whose recursivity, for a recursive Prüfer domain
R
,
is equivalent to the decidability
of the theory of
R
-modules of size
n
, uniformly in
n
. This is proved in Theorem
7.6
.
The main feature of
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
is that it is often easier to check in examples that
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
is recursive than it is to check that the theory of modules of size
n
is decidable uniformly in
n
.

The set
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
is a generalisation of
\mathrm{PP}(R)
, which is defined in
[
GLT19
]
and inspired by the characterisation of commutative von Neumann regular rings with decidable theories of modules given in
[
PP88
]
. In
[
GLT19
]
, for a recursive Bézout domain
R
, under the condition that for each maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
,
R_{\mathfrak{m}}
has dense value group, it is shown that the theory of
R
-modules is decidable if and only if
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
and
\mathrm{PP}(R)
are recursive. Building heavily on
[
GLT19
]
, we show, that
this result also holds for Prüfer domains.

Theorem
3.12
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain such that
R_{\mathfrak{m}}
has dense value group for all maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
. The theory of
R
-modules is decidable if and only if
\mathrm{PP}(R)
and
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive.

It follows from this result that the theory of modules of a recursive Prüfer domain with dense value groups (or infinite residue fields) is decidable if and only if there is an algorithm deciding inclusions of Ziegler basic open sets and the theory of finite modules of size
n
is decidable uniformly in
n
. The same result for commutative von Neumann regular rings easily follows from
[
PP88
]
. This does not appear to be the case for arbitrary Prüfer domains.

The third set,
X(R)
, captures information about finite Baur-Monk invariants of the, in some sense intrinsically infinite modules,
R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I
where
\mathfrak{p}\lhd R
is a prime ideal and
I\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Main Theorem
11.1
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain. The theory of
R
-modules is decidable if and only if the sets
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
,
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and
X(R)
are recursive.

Our characterisation is such that it can be easily checked for concrete rings. We illustrate this in section
12

by using our main theorem to show that the ring of integer valued polynomials with rational coefficients,
\text{Int}(\mathbb{Z})
, has decidable theory of modules.
In order to prove that
\mathrm{DPR}(\text{Int}(\mathbb{Z}))
,
\mathrm{EPP}(\text{Int}(\mathbb{Z}))
and
X(\text{Int}(\mathbb{Z}))
are recursive, we use Ax’s result,
[
Ax68
, Thm 17]
, that the common theory of the
p
-adic valued fields
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
as
p
varies, is decidable.

Section
1
contains background material and simple preparations for the rest of the paper. Its main purpose is to make the article as
accessible as possible. We postpone a guide to the proof and discussion of what happens in each section to subsection
2.4
.

When it doesn’t complicate the proofs, we will state some of our intermediate results for arithmetical rings i.e. commutative rings whose localisations at prime ideals are valuation rings.

2.
Preliminaries

Notation: In this article
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
,
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\mathbb{N}_{n}=\{m\geq n\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{P}
denotes the set of prime natural numbers. For a ring
R
, let
\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
denote the category of (right) modules.

2.1.
Model theory of Modules

For general background on model theory of modules see
[
Pre88
]
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
\mathcal{L}_{R}:=\{0,+,(\cdot r)_{r\in R}\}
be the language of (right)
R
-modules and
T_{R}
be the theory of (right)
R
-modules. A (right) pp-
n
-formula is a formula of the form

\exists y_{1},\ldots,y_{l}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{l}y_{i}r_{ij}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}s_{ij}=0

where
r_{ij},s_{ij}\in R
. For
a\in R
, we write
a|x
for the pp-
1
-formula
\exists y\,x=ya
.

The solution set
\varphi(M)
of a pp-
n
-formula
\varphi
in an
R
-module
M
is a subgroup of
M^{n}
. For
\varphi,\psi
, pp-
n
-formulae and
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
, we will write
\psi\leq_{M}\varphi
to mean that
\psi(M)\subseteq\varphi(M)
. We will write
\psi\leq\varphi
to mean that
\psi\leq_{M}\varphi
for all
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
. After identifying equivalent pp-
n
-formulae, the set of pp-
n
-formulae,
\textnormal{pp}_{R}^{n}
, equipped with the order
\leq
is a lattice.

A
pp-
m
-pair
will simply mean a pair of pp-
m
-formulae and we will write
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}
for such pairs. We write
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}(M)
for the quotient group
\varphi(M)/\varphi(M)\cap\psi(M)
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and pp-pair
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}
, there is a sentence, denoted
\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right|\geq n
, in the language of (right)
R
-modules, which expresses, in every
R
-module
M
, that
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}(M)
has at least
n
elements. We write
\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right|=n
for the sentence which expresses, in every
R
-module
M
, that
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}(M)
has exactly
n
elements.

Theorem 2.1

(Baur-Monk)
.

Let
R
be a ring. Every sentence in
\mathcal{L}_{R}
is equivalent to a boolean combination of sentences of the form
\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right|\geq n
where
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}
is a pp-
1
-pair.

An embedding
f:A\rightarrow B
is
pure
if for all pp-
1
-formulae
\varphi
and
m\in A
,
f(m)\in\varphi(B)
implies
m\in\varphi(A)
. A module
N
is
pure-injective
if for every embedding
f:A\rightarrow B
and homomorphism
g:A\rightarrow N
, there exists
h:B\rightarrow N
such that
h\circ f=g
.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
Pre88
, 4.36]

Let
R
be a ring. For all
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
, there exist indecomposable pure-injective modules
N_{i}\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
for
i\in I
such that
\oplus_{i\in I}N_{i}
is elementary equivalent to
M
.

We say a pure-embedding
i:M\rightarrow N
with
N
pure-injective is a
pure-injective hull
of
M
if for every other pure-embedding
g:M\rightarrow K
where
K
is pure-injective, there is a pure-embedding
h:N\rightarrow K
such that
h\circ i=g
. The pure-injective hull of
M
is unique up to isomorphism over
M
and we will write
H(M)
for any module
N
such that the inclusion of
M
in
N
is a pure-injective hull of
M
. Every module is a elementary substructure of its pure-injective hull
[
Pre88
, 2.27]
. So, in particular every module is elementary equivalent to its pure-injective hull.

The (right)
Ziegler spectrum
, denoted
\textnormal{Zg}_{R}
, is a topological space whose points are isomorphism classes of indecomposable pure injective (right) modules and which has a basis of compact open sets given by

\left(\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right):=\{N\in\textnormal{pinj}_{R}\ |\ \varphi(N)\supsetneq\varphi(N)\cap\psi(N)\}

where
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}
range over pp-
1
-pairs.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, Prest gave a lattice anti-isomorphism
D:\textnormal{pp}_{R}^{n}\rightarrow{}_{R}\textnormal{pp}^{n}
(see
[
Pre88
, 8.21]
). As is standard, we denote its inverse
{}_{R}\textnormal{pp}^{n}\rightarrow\textnormal{pp}_{R}^{n}
also by
D
. We don’t recall the full definition of
D
here but instead note that for all
a\in R
,
Da|x
is
ax=0
and
D(xa=0)
is
a|x
.

Herzog extended this duality to an isomorphism between the lattice of open sets of the right and left Ziegler spectra of a ring
[
Her93
, 4.4]
, and, to a useful bijection between the complete theories of right and left
R
-modules.

The following proposition is direct consequence of
[
Her93
, 6.6]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
R
be a ring. Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
n\leq m
and for
1\leq i\leq m
, let
N_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}
be a pp-pair. For

{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|=N_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{i=n+1}^{m}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|\geq N_{i},

define

D{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left|\nicefrac{{D\psi_{i}}}{{D\varphi_{i}}}\right|=N_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{i=n+1}^{m}\left|\nicefrac{{D\psi_{i}}}{{D\varphi_{i}}}\right|\geq N_{i}.

There exists a right
R
-module satisfying
\chi
if and only if there exits a left
R
-module satisfying
D{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
.

A priori, duality may not appear particularly relevant to an article about commutative rings. However, its use significantly simplifies some of the proofs in this paper and, as in
[
Gre15
]
, the fact that it exchanges formulae
xb=0
with
b|x
allows us to reduce the number of calculations.

2.2.
Decidability and recursive Prüfer domains

A
recursive ring
is either a finite ring or a ring
R
together with a bijection
\pi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow R
such that addition and multiplication in
R
induce recursive functions on
\mathbb{N}
via
\pi
.

Note that if
R
is a ring and
\pi:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow R
is a bijection then
T_{R}
is recursively axiomatisable with respect to
\pi
if and only if
R
together with
\pi
is a recursive ring.

When proving decidability results about theories of modules, it is common to work with an “effectively given” ring rather than just a recursive one. Usually, a ring of a particular type is called effectively given if
R
is a recursive ring and the bijection
\pi
satisfies some extra conditions which are equivalent, for that particular type of ring, to Prest’s condition
(D)
holding (see
[
Pre88
, pg 334]
). Recall that a recursive ring satisfies condition
(D)
if there is an algorithm which, given
\varphi,\psi\in\textnormal{pp}_{R}^{1}
answers whether
\psi\leq\varphi
.
For example, a recursive valuation domain
V
is said to be
effectively given
if the preimage under
\pi
of the set of units of
V
is recursive. A recursive Prüfer domain
R
is said to be
effectively given
if the preimage under
\pi
of the set of
(a,b)\in R^{2}
such that
a\in bR
is recursive.

Whether one works with recursive rings or effectively given ones is largely a matter of personal taste. For simplicity, we choose to work with recursive rings.

It was remarked in
[
GLT19
, paragraph before 2.4]
, that the property that
a\in bR
is recursive is never used in
[
GLPT18
]
and
[
GLT19
]
. Moreover, see
[
GLT19
, 2.4]
, if
R
is a recursive Prüfer domain and the set
\mathrm{DPR}(R)\subseteq R^{4}
is recursive then
a\in bR
is recursive. In particular, even though they are stated for effectively given Prüfer domains, all results in
[
GLPT18
]
and
[
GLT19
]
in fact hold for recursive Prüfer domains.

The next theorem is a well-known and easy to prove consequence of the Baur-Monk Theorem. Note that since
T_{R}
is recursively axiomatisable when
R
is recursive, given a sentence
\chi
in
\mathcal{L}_{R}
, we can always find, using a proof algorithm, a sentence
{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}^{\prime}
as in the statement of the Baur-Monk theorem which is
T_{R}
-equivalent to
\chi
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
R
be a recursive ring. The theory of
R
-modules is decidable if and only if there is an algorithm which, given a sentence
\chi
of the form

{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|=N_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{i=n+1}^{m}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|\geq N_{i},

where
N_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}
is a pp-
1
-pair for
1\leq i\leq m
, answers whether there exists an
R
-module satisfying
\chi
.

Remark 2.5
.

Let
R
be a recursive ring. There is an algorithm deciding inclusions of Ziegler basic open sets if and only if there is an algorithm which, given a sentence

{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|\geq E_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}\left|\nicefrac{{\sigma_{j}}}{{\tau_{j}}}\right|=1,

where
\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}},\nicefrac{{\sigma_{j}}}{{\tau_{j}}}
are pp-
1
-pairs and
E_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
, answers whether there exists an
R
-module satisfying
\chi
.

Proof.

There is a module satisfying
\chi
as in the statement if and only if for each
1\leq i\leq n
, there exists an indecomposable pure-injective module
N_{i}
such that
N_{i}\in\left(\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right)\backslash\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\left(\nicefrac{{\sigma_{j}}}{{\tau_{j}}}\right)
. This is a standard argument. For the forward direction use
2.2
. For the reverse, observe
\oplus_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}^{E_{i}}
satisfies
\chi
.
∎

2.3.
Arithmetical rings and Prüfer domains

A commutative ring is
arithmetical
[1]
[1]

[1]

This condition is often referred to as Prüfer in papers on Model Theory of Modules. However, algebraists tend to use the term Prüfer for the stronger condition that every regular ideal is invertible. To avoid confusion we choose the term with a unique definition.
if all its localisations at maximal ideals are valuation rings. Equivalently,
[
Jen66
, Thm 1]
, a commutative ring
R
is arithmetical if its lattice of ideals is distributive. A
Prüfer domain
is an integral domain which is arithmetical.

The following is a consequence of
[
Tug03
, 1.3]
.

Lemma 2.6
.

If
R
is an arithmetical ring then for all
a,b\in R
, there exist
\alpha,r,s\in R
such that
a\alpha=br
and
b(\alpha-1)=as
.

Note that if
R
is a recursive arithmetical ring then there is an algorithm which, given
a,b\in R
, finds
\alpha,r,s
satisfying the above equations. We will frequently use this fact without note.

An
R
-module is
pp-uniserial
if its lattice of pp-definable subgroups is totally ordered. Over a commutative ring all pp-definable subgroups are submodules. Thus, all uniserial modules over a commutative ring are pp-uniserial.

The lattice of pp-
1
-formulae of a commutative ring is distributive if and only if it is arithmetical
[
EH95
, 3.1]
. Thus, the following is a direct consequence of
[
Pun03
, 3.3]
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. All indecomposable pure-injective
R
-modules are pp-uniserial if and only if
R
is arithmetical.

The endomorphism rings of indecomposable pure-injective modules are local
[
Pre09
, 4.3.43]
. Therefore, if
R
is a commutative ring and
N
is an indecomposable pure-injective module then the set,
\textnormal{Att}N
, of
r\in R
acting on
N
non-bijectively form a prime ideal.
Thus, if
N
is an indecomposable pure-injective module over a commutative ring
R
then
N
may be equipped with the structure of an
R_{\textnormal{Att}N}
-module. Moreover,
N
remains indecomposable and pure-injective as an
R_{\textnormal{Att}N}
-module. Conversely, if
N
is an indecomposable pure-injective
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
-module for some prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\lhd R
then the restriction of
N
to
R
remains indecomposable and pure-injective.

The following lemma is now an easy consequence of the fact that indecomposable pure-injective modules over arithmetical rings are pp-uniserial (a proof appears in
[
GLT19
, 2.8]
).

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
R
be an arithmetical ring and
N
an indecomposable pure-injective
R
-module. The sets

\textnormal{Div}N:=\{r\in R\ |\ Nr\subsetneq N\},

\textnormal{Ass}N:=\{r\in R\ |\ \text{ there exists }m\in N\backslash\{0\}\text{ such that }mr=0\}

and

\textnormal{Att}N:=\textnormal{Div}N\cup\textnormal{Ass}N

are prime ideals.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
R
be an arithmetical ring and
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
.

(1)

For all
\alpha\in R
, there exist
M_{1},M_{2}\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
such that
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\equiv M
,
\left|\nicefrac{{x\alpha=0}}{{x=0}}(M_{1})\right|=1
,
\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{\alpha|x}}(M_{1})\right|=1
,
\left|\nicefrac{{x(\alpha-1)=0}}{{x=0}}(M_{2})\right|=1
and
\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{(\alpha-1)|x}}(M_{2})\right|=1
.

(2)

For all
a,b\in R
, there exist
M_{1},M_{2}\in M
such that
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\equiv M
,
\left|\nicefrac{{ab|x}}{{x=0}}(M_{1})\right|=1
and
\left|\nicefrac{{xa=0}}{{b|x}}(M_{2})\right|=1
.

Proof.

(1) By
2.2
, there exist indecomposable pure-injective
R
-modules
N_{i}
for
i\in I
such that
M\equiv\oplus_{i\in I}N_{i}
. Since
\textnormal{Att}N_{i}
is a proper ideal for each
N_{i}
, for all
i\in I
, either
\alpha\notin\textnormal{Att}N_{i}
or
\alpha-1\notin\textnormal{Att}N_{i}
. Let
I_{\alpha}
be the set of
i\in I
such that
\alpha\notin\textnormal{Att}N_{i}
and let
I_{\alpha-1}=I\backslash I_{\alpha}
. So, for all
i\in I_{\alpha-1}
,
\alpha-1\notin\textnormal{Att}N_{i}
.
For each
\beta\in R
and
N
indecomposable pure-injective,
\beta\notin\textnormal{Att}N
if and only if
\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{\beta|x}}(N)\right|=1
and
\left|\nicefrac{{x\beta=0}}{{x=0}}(M_{1})\right|=1
. Therefore
\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{\alpha|x}}(\oplus_{i\in I_{\alpha}}N_{i})\right|=1
,
\left|\nicefrac{{x\alpha=0}}{{x=0}}(\oplus_{i\in I_{\alpha}}N_{i})\right|=1
,
\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{(\alpha-1)|x}}(\oplus_{i\in I_{\alpha-1}}N_{i})\right|=1
and
\left|\nicefrac{{x(\alpha-1)=0}}{{x=0}}(\oplus_{i\in I_{\alpha-1}}N_{i})\right|=1
.

(2) For any
L\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
,
xb=0\geq_{L}a|x
if and only if
ab\in\textnormal{ann}_{R}L
. So
\left|\nicefrac{{ab|x}}{{x=0}}(L)\right|=1
if and only if
xb=0\geq_{L}a|x
. Let
N
be an indecomposable pure-injective
R
-module. By
2.7
, either
xb=0\geq_{N}a|x
or
a|x\geq_{N}xb=0
. So either
\left|\nicefrac{{ab|x}}{{x=0}}(N)\right|=1
or
\left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{a|x}}(N)\right|=1
. The proof is now as in
(1)
.
∎

It is easy to see that if
R
is a commutative ring,
\mathfrak{p}\lhd R
is a prime ideal and
M
is an
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
-module then the restriction to
R
of the pure-injective hull of
M
as an
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
-module is equal to the pure-injective hull of
M
as an
R
-module.

Recall that a module is called
uniserial
if its lattice of submodules form a chain.

Theorem 2.10
.

[
Zie84
]

Let
V
be a valuation domain with field of fractions
Q
. Every indecomposable pure-injective
V
-module is the pure-injective hull of a module
J/I
where
I\subsetneq J\subseteq Q
are submodules of
Q
.

So, in particular, over a valuation domain, all indecomposable pure-injective
R
-modules are pure-injective hulls of uniserial modules. It is not known if all indecomposable pure-injective modules over valuation rings are pure-injective hulls of uniserial modules (see
[
EH95
, §4]
).

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
R
be a Prüfer domain. For any sentence
{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}\in\mathcal{L}_{R}
, there exists
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
such that
M\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
if and only if there exist
n\in\mathbb{N}
, prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\lhd R
and uniserial
R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
-modules
U_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\oplus_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
.

Proof.

For any ring
R
, there exists
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
such that
M\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
if and only if there exist
n\in\mathbb{N}
and indecomposable pure-injective
R
-modules
N_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\oplus_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
. The result now follows from
2.10
.
∎

We will frequently use the following easy lemma.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
V
be a valuation domain,
\varphi
a pp-
1
-formula and
U
a uniserial
V
-module. If
\left|U/\varphi(U)\right|
is finite but not equal to
1
then
U\cong V/I
for some ideal
I\lhd V
.

Proof.

Since
U
is uniserial, so is
U/\varphi(U)
. Therefore, since
U/\varphi(U)
is finite, there exists
u\in U
such that
u+\varphi(U)
generates
U/\varphi(U)
as a
V
-module. Since
U/\varphi(U)\neq 0
,
uR\supseteq\varphi(U)
. Therefore
uR=U
.
∎

We finish this subsection by reviewing material about ideals of valuation domains.

For any commutative ring
R
,
r\in R
and ideal
I\lhd R
, define

(I:r):=\{a\in R\ |\ ar\in I\}.

Note that
(I:r)
is an ideal of
R
.

Definition 2.13
.

For
V
a valuation domain and
I\lhd R
a proper ideal, define
I^{\#}:=\bigcup_{a\notin I}(I:a)
. By convention, we define
V^{\#}
to be the unique maximal ideal of
V
.

Note that this definition agrees with the definition given in
[
FS01
, ChII §4]
, that is, for
I\neq 0
,
r\in I^{\#}
if and only if
rI\subsetneq I
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
V
be a valuation domain.

(1)

For any ideal
I\lhd V
,
I^{\#}
is a prime ideal.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{p}\lhd V
is a prime ideal then
\mathfrak{p}^{\#}=\mathfrak{p}
.

(3)

If
I\lhd V
and
a\in V\backslash\{0\}
then
(aI)^{\#}=I^{\#}
.

(4)

If
I\lhd V
and
V/I
is finite then
I^{\#}
is the unique maximal ideal of
V
.

Proof.

The first
3
statements are in
[
FS01
, II.4]
. We prove (iv). If
I=V
then the statement follows directly from the definition. Otherwise,
I\subseteq I^{\#}
and hence
V/I^{\#}
is also finite. Since
V/I^{\#}
is a finite integral domain, it is a field. Therefore
I^{\#}
is maximal.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
R
be a Prüfer domain,
\mathfrak{p}\lhd R
a prime ideal and
I\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Then, for all
\delta,\gamma\in R
,

(1)

\left|\nicefrac{{x\delta=0}}{{x=0}}(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I)\right|=1
if and only if
\delta\notin I^{\#}
or
I=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and,

(2)

\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{\gamma|x}}(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I)\right|=1
if and only if
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{p}
or
I=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Proof.

(1) For any
\delta\in R
,
\left|\nicefrac{{x\delta=0}}{{x=0}}(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I)\right|=1
if and only if
(I:\delta)\subseteq I
. Now
(I:\delta)\subseteq I
if and only if
I=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
, or, for all
a\notin I
,
\delta a\notin I
. Therefore
\left|\nicefrac{{x\delta=0}}{{x=0}}(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I)\right|=1
if and only if
\delta\notin I^{\#}
or
I=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(2) For any
\gamma\in R
,
\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{\gamma|x}}(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I)\right|=1
if and only if
\gamma R_{\mathfrak{p}}+I=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
. This is true if and only if
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{p}
or
I=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

Let
b\in R\backslash\{0\}
,
\mathfrak{p}\lhd R
be a prime ideal and
I\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}}
be an ideal. If
R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I
is finite then
\left|I/bI\right|=\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/bR_{\mathfrak{p}}\right|
.

Proof.

Since
b\neq 0
,

\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I\right|\cdot\left|I/bI\right|=\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/bI\right|=\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/bR_{\mathfrak{p}}\right|\cdot\left|bR_{\mathfrak{p}}/bI\right|=\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/bR_{\mathfrak{p}}\right|\cdot\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I\right|.

So, since
\left|R/I\right|
is non-zero,
\left|I/bI\right|=\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/bR_{\mathfrak{p}}\right|
.
∎

We will frequently use the following lemma which becomes particularly useful when
R
is a valuation ring because then for all
r\in R
and
I\lhd R
either
r\in I
or
rR\supseteq I
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
r\in R
and
I\lhd R
. Then
rR\supseteq I
if and only there exists
J\lhd R
such that
I=rJ
.

Proof.

The reverse direction is clear. For the forward direction, take
J=(I:r)
.
∎

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
V
be a valuation domain,
I,J\lhd V
and
a\in R\backslash\{0\}
. Then
J\supseteq(I:a)
if and only
aJ\supseteq I
or
J=V
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose
J\supseteq(I:a)
. Since
V
is a valuation domain, either
a\in I
or
aV\supseteq I
. If
a\in I
then
J\supseteq(I:a)=V
. So
J=V
. Suppose
aV\supseteq I
. Take
c\in I
. There exists
b\in V
such that
ab=c
. So
b\in(I:a)\subseteq J
. Hence
c=ab\in aJ
as required.

(\Leftarrow)
If
J=V
then
J\supseteq(I:a)
. So, suppose
aJ\supseteq I
. Take
c\in(I:a)
. Then
ac\in I\supseteq aJ
. Since
a\neq 0
,
c\in J
. Therefore
J\supseteq(I:a)
.
∎

2.4.
Guide to the proof

The reverse direction of the main theorem is proved in
3
. That is, we show that if
T_{R}
is decidable then
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
,
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and
X(R)
are recursive (see
[
GLPT18
, 6.4]
(or
3.5
),
3.18
and
3.22
).

The proof of the forward direction of the main theorem has
3
principal ingredients.

(1)

Consequences of
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
,
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and
X(R)
being recursive. (§
3
and §
7
)

(2)

Syntactic reductions. (§
4
, §
5
and §
9
)

(3)

Semantic input. (§
6
, §
8
and §
10
)

(A) In section
3
we introduce and analyse the sets
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
,
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and
X(R)
. These sets are chosen to be as simple as possible so that our theorem is as easy as possible to apply to concrete rings. For this reason, work needs to be done to obtain more elaborate consequences of them being recursive.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a set
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
was introduced in
[
GLPT18
]
. We show that,
3.7
, for
R
a recursive arithmetical ring, if
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive then the sets
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
are recursive (uniformly in
n
). Combining this with
[
GLPT18
, 7.1]
, or more precisely its proof, we conclude that we can effectively decide inclusions of Ziegler basic open sets if and only if
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive.

In section
7
, we investigate the consequences of
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
being recursive, and of

\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and the radical relation being recursive (which is deployed in section
8
).
We show that for a recursive Prüfer domain the theory of
R
-modules of size
n
is decidable
uniformly in
n
if and only if
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
is recursive,
7.6
.

In the proof of the forward direction of the main theorem, the set
X(R)
is only ever used in section
10
.

(B) Given a sentence
\chi
as in (
2.4
) (from
2.4
) we often produce a finite set
S
of tuples of sentences
({\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{1},\ldots,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{n})
with each
{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{i}
having a “better” form than
\chi
such that there exists
M\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
if and only if there exist
({\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{1},\ldots,{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{n})\in S
and modules
M_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
with
M_{i}\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{i}
. This is roughly what happens in the proof of
[
GLT19
, 4.1]
.
Section
4
introduces two important formalisms (and ideas) which used in combination are key to the proof. Essentially they allow us to “automate” some reductions similar to those in the proof of
[
GLPT18
, 4.1]
which in this article become too complicated to perform entirely by hand.

It is shown in
[
GLT19
, 4.1]
that for arithmetical rings
[2]
[2]

[2]

The result is stated there only for Prüfer domains but the same proof implies the result for all arithmetical rings (see section
5
).
it is enough to consider sentences like (
2.4
) where the pp-pairs involved are of the form
\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}
or
\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}
. Section
5
uses the formalisms in section
4
, to show that it is enough to consider sentences like (
2.4
) where at most one conjunct of the form
\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=D
or
\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|\geq D
with
D\geq 2
occurs and where at most one conjunct of the form
\left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}\right|=G
or
\left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}\right|\geq G
with
G\geq 2
and
b,c\neq 0
occurs.

In section
9
a notion of complexity, called the extended signature, is put on the set
W
of sentences as in (
2.4
) as reduced to in section
5
. The set of extended signatures is equipped with an artinian partial order.
The reduction processes in section
9
terminate at expressions whose extended signatures are not reducible.
Some of the sentences which are not reducible are of a form for which we can answer whether there exists a module satisfying them, because
there is an algorithm deciding inclusions of Ziegler basic open sets.
The remaining sentences are of a particular simple form and we deal with them in section
10
.

(C) In section
6
, for pp-pairs
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}
of the form
\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}
,
\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}
with
b,c\neq 0
and
\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c|x}}
with
c\neq 0
, we give a description of the uniserial module
U
over a valuation domain
V
, such that
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}(U)
is finite but non-zero. Unlike the descriptions of such modules in
[
Gre15
]
and
[
PPT07
]
, the results we prove do not depend on whether the value group of
V
is dense or not.
We now describe how we use semantic input to deal with sentences as in (
2.4
) with a conjunct of the form

\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=D
, or of the form
\left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}\right|=G
where
b,c\neq 0
.

For instance, if
\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}(U)
is finite but non-zero then it is easy to show that
U\cong V/dI
for some ideal
I\lhd V
. In view of
2.11
, for
R
a Prüfer domain, this means that for
\chi
a sentence as in (
2.4
),
D\in\mathbb{N}_{2}
and
d\in R\backslash\{0\}
, there exists
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
such that
M\models\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=D\wedge{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
if and only if there exist
h\in\mathbb{N}
, prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\lhd R
and ideals
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq h
, and,
M^{\prime}\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
with
\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}(M^{\prime})\right|=1
such that
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/dI_{i}\oplus M^{\prime}\models\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=D\wedge{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
.

In section
8
, we show that if
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and the radical relation are recursive then there is an algorithm which given
D\in\mathbb{N}
,
d\in R\backslash\{0\}
and a sentence
\Theta
as in (
2.4
), answers whether there exists a direct sum
\oplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/dI_{i}
satisfying
\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=D\wedge\Theta
. This is used in
9.5
to produce sentences
{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{1},\ldots{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{n}
as in (
2.4
) such that there exists an
R
-module satisfying
\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=D\wedge{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
if and only if there exists an
R
-module satisfying
\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=1\wedge{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
.
The sentences
{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}_{i}
are less complex than
\chi
in a way precisely defined in section
9
.

Similar, but slightly more complicated, reductions are made for pp-pairs of the form
\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}
where
b,c\neq 0
.

For pp-pairs of the form
\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c|x}}
we need to do something different. It is easy to see,
6.3
, that if
U
is a uniserial module over a valuation domain
V
then
\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c|x}}(U)
is finite but non-zero if and only if
U\cong V/cI
for some
I\lhd V
or
U
is finite and
c\in\textnormal{ann}_{R}U
.
However, it does not seem possible, in this case, to make a reduction as for
\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=D
and sums of modules of the form
R_{\mathfrak{p}}/dI
. This is the main reason that we need to make the syntactic reductions in section
5
and
9
. In particular, the set of sentences that are not reducible in the sense of section
9
, contains only a small number of forms of sentences with a conjunct of the form
\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c|x}}\right|=C
. These sentences are considered individually in section
10
.

3.
Recursive sets

In this section we consider the sets
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
,
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
and
X(R)
. In each case, we show that if
T_{R}
is decidable then they are recursive.

3.1.
The set
\mathrm{DPR}(R)

In
[
GLPT18
]
, a family of relations
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
were defined. Although not directly stated there, see
[
GLPT18
, 7.1]
, it was shown that, for
R
a recursive
[3]
[3]

[3]

Recall
R
recursive and
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
recursive imply
R
is effectively given
Prüfer domain, if the sets
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
are recursive (uniformly in
n
) then there is an algorithm deciding inclusions of finite unions of Ziegler basic open sets. However, it was not known if this condition was necessary for the existence of such an algorithm, or even if the decidability of the theory of modules of Prüfer domain implied that
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
is recursive for any
n>1
. It is a consequence of
3.5
that the existence of an algorithm deciding inclusions of finite unions of Ziegler basic open sets implies that the sets
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
are recursive (uniformly in
n
).

For a recursive Bézout domain, it was shown that if
\mathrm{DPR}(R):=\mathrm{DPR}_{1}(R)
is recursive then there is an algorithm deciding inclusions of finite unions of Ziegler basic open sets. For Prüfer domains, it was not known if
\mathrm{DPR}_{1}(R)
being recursive is sufficient to imply that there is an algorithm deciding inclusions of finite unions of Ziegler basic open sets. We show,
3.7
, that, for
R
a Prüfer domain,
\mathrm{DPR}_{1}(R)
recursive implies
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
n
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring.

•

For each
l\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathrm{DPR}_{l}(R)
be the set of
2l+2
-tuples
(a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{l},c,d_{1},\raisebox{-3.0pt}{$\cdot$},d_{l})\in R^{2l+2}
such that, for all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\lhd R
with
\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q}\neq R
, either
a\in\mathfrak{p}
,
c\in\mathfrak{q}
,
b_{i}\notin\mathfrak{p}
for some
1\leq i\leq l
or
d_{i}\notin\mathfrak{q}
for some
1\leq i\leq l
.

•

Let
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
be the set of
4
-tuples
(a,B,c,D)
, where
a,c\in R
and
B,D\lhd R
are finitely generated ideals, such that for all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\lhd R
with
\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q}\neq R
, either
a\in\mathfrak{p}
,
c\in\mathfrak{q}
,
B\nsubseteq\mathfrak{p}
or
D\nsubseteq\mathfrak{q}
.

Note that
(a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{l},c,d_{1},\ldots,d_{l})\in\mathrm{DPR}_{l}(R)
if and only if

(a,\sum_{i=1}^{l}b_{i}R,c,\sum_{i=1}^{l}d_{i}R)\in\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R).

In the following lemma, for
R
a commutative ring,
I\lhd R
an ideal and
X\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}R
, let
V(I)
denote the closed set, in the Zariski topology, of prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}
such that
\mathfrak{p}\supseteq I
and
\overline{X}
the closure of
X
in the Zariski topology.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
a\in R
and
B\lhd R
. Then

(\textnormal{rad}B:a)=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{p}\in\textnormal{Spec}R\\
\mathfrak{p}\supseteq B,\ a\notin\mathfrak{p}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{p}.

Hence,

V((\textnormal{rad}B:a))=\overline{V(B)\backslash V(aR)}.

Proof.

Suppose
r\in(\textnormal{rad}B:a)
. Then
(ra)^{n}\in B
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}\lhd R
be a prime ideal with
B\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
and
a\notin\mathfrak{p}
. Then
r^{n}a^{n}=(ra)^{n}\in\mathfrak{p}
. Therefore
r\in\mathfrak{p}
.

Conversely, suppose that
r\notin(\textnormal{rad}B:a)
i.e.
(ra)^{n}\notin B
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A standard argument using Zorn’s lemma produces a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\lhd R
such that
\mathfrak{p}\supseteq B
and
(ra)^{n}\notin\mathfrak{p}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Now
(ra)^{n}\notin\mathfrak{p}
implies
r\notin\mathfrak{p}
and
a\notin\mathfrak{p}
. So we have proved the first statement. For any
X\subseteq\textnormal{Spec}R
,
V(\bigcap_{\mathfrak{p}\in X}\mathfrak{p})=\overline{X}
. Therefore, the second statement follows from the first.
∎

The following statement with
\mathrm{DPR}(R):=\mathrm{DPR}_{1}(R)
in place of
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
is proved in
[
GLPT18
, 6.3]
for Prüfer domains. We use
3.2
to further extend it to all commutative rings.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. The following are equivalent for
a,c\in R
and
B,D\lhd R
finitely generated.

(1)

(a,B,c,D)\in\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)

(2)

1\in(\textnormal{rad}(B):a)+(\textnormal{rad}(D):c)

Proof.

We prove this proposition topologically.

For all
a,c\in R
and
B,D\lhd R
,
1\in(\textnormal{rad}(B):a)+(\textnormal{rad}(D):c)
if and only if
V((\textnormal{rad}B:a))\cap V((\textnormal{rad}D:c))=\emptyset
. By
3.2
,
V((\textnormal{rad}B:a))=\overline{V(B)\backslash V(aR)}
and
V((\textnormal{rad}D:c))=\overline{V(D)\backslash V(cR)}
. Thus

1\in(\textnormal{rad}(B):a)+(\textnormal{rad}(D):c)

if and only if

\overline{V(B)\backslash V(aR)}\cap\overline{V(D)\backslash V(cR)}=\emptyset.

Now, by
[
DST19
, 1.5.4 (i)]
,

\overline{V(B)\backslash V(aR)}=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{p}\in V(B)\backslash V(aR)}V(\mathfrak{p})\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \overline{V(D)\backslash V(cR)}=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{q}\in V(D)\backslash V(cR)}V(\mathfrak{q}).

So
1\in(\textnormal{rad}(B):a)+(\textnormal{rad}(D):c)
if and only if

(
\dagger
)

(\bigcup_{\mathfrak{p}\in V(B)\backslash V(aR)}V(\mathfrak{p}))\cap(\bigcup_{\mathfrak{q}\in V(D)\backslash V(cR)}V(\mathfrak{q}))=\emptyset.

Now (

†

‣
3.1
) holds if and only if for all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
such that
a\notin\mathfrak{p}
,
B\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
,
c\notin\mathfrak{q}
and
D\subseteq\mathfrak{q}
, we have
V(\mathfrak{p})\cap V(\mathfrak{q})=\emptyset
(i.e.
\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q}=R
).
∎

We refer to the relation
a\in\textnormal{rad}bR
as the
radical relation
.

Remark 3.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. For
a,b\in R
,
a\in\textnormal{rad}bR
if and only if
(a,b,a,b)\in\mathrm{DPR}(R)
. In particular, if
R
is a recursive ring and
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive then the radical relation is recursive.

For
R
a commutative ring and
B,D\lhd R
finitely generated ideals, let
xB=0
denote the pp-formula
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}xb_{i}=0
where
B=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}R
and let
D|x
denote the pp-formula
\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}|x
where
D=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}R
. Note that, up to
T_{R}
equivalence, these formulae don’t depend on the choice of generators of
B
and
D
.

The following statement with
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
replaced by
\mathrm{DPR}(R)=\mathrm{DPR}_{1}(R)
is proved in
[
GLPT18
, 6.4]
for Prüfer domains.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
R
be an arithmetical ring. The following are equivalent for
a,c\in R
and
B,D\lhd R
finitely generated ideals.

(1)

(a,B,c,D)\in\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)

(2)

\left(\nicefrac{{xB=0}}{{D|x}}\right)\subseteq\left(\nicefrac{{xa=0}}{{x=0}}\right)\cup\left(\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c|x}}\right)

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
: Suppose
(1)
holds. By
3.3
,
1\in(\textnormal{rad}B:a)+(\textnormal{rad}D:c)
. Hence there exist
u\in R
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(ua)^{n}\in B
and
((u-1)c)^{n}\in D
.

Suppose
N\in\left(\nicefrac{{xB=0}}{{D|x}}\right)
. Take
m\in N
such that
mb=0
for all
b\in B
and
m\notin ND
. So
mu^{n}a^{n}=0
and
m\notin N(u-1)^{n}c^{n}
. Since
\text{Att}N
is a proper ideal, either
u\notin\text{Att}N
or
u-1\notin\text{Att}N
. Suppose
u\notin\text{Att}N
. Then
mu^{n}a^{n}=0
implies
ma^{n}=0
and hence
N\in\left(\nicefrac{{xa^{n}=0}}{{x=0}}\right)=\left(\nicefrac{{xa=0}}{{x=0}}\right)
as required. Now suppose
u-1\notin\text{Att}N
. Then
N(u-1)^{n}c^{n}=Nc^{n}
and hence
N\in\left(\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c^{n}|x}}\right)=\left(\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c|x}}\right)
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
: Suppose
(2)
holds. Note that if
\left(\nicefrac{{xB=0}}{{D|x}}\right)\subseteq\left(\nicefrac{{xa=0}}{{x=0}}\right)\cup\left(\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c|x}}\right)
then, applying Herzog’s duality,
\left(\nicefrac{{xD=0}}{{B|x}}\right)\subseteq\left(\nicefrac{{xc=0}}{{x=0}}\right)\cup\left(\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{a|x}}\right)
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
are prime ideals with
\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q}\neq R
. Further, suppose that
a\notin\mathfrak{p}
,
B\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
and
D\subseteq\mathfrak{q}
. We need to show that
c\in\mathfrak{q}
.

Since
R
is an arithmetical ring, either
\mathfrak{p}\supseteq\mathfrak{q}
or
\mathfrak{q}\supseteq\mathfrak{p}
. Suppose
\mathfrak{p}\supseteq\mathfrak{q}
. Then, again since
R
is an arithmetical ring,
R_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{q}R_{\mathfrak{p}}
is uniserial and its pure-injective hull
N:=H(R_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{q}R_{\mathfrak{p}})
is indecomposable. Since
D\subseteq\mathfrak{q}
,
xD=0
is equivalent to
x=x
in
R_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{q}R_{\mathfrak{p}}
and hence in
N
. Since
B\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
,
B|x
is not equivalent to
x=x
in
R_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathfrak{q}R_{\mathfrak{p}}
and hence in
N
. Thus
N\in\left(\nicefrac{{xD=0}}{{B|x}}\right)
. Since
a\notin\mathfrak{p}
,
N\notin\left(\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{a|x}}\right)
and hence
N\notin\left(\nicefrac{{xc=0}}{{x=0}}\right)
. Therefore
c\notin\mathfrak{q}
, for otherwise
xc=0
is equivalent to
x=0
in
N
. The argument when
\mathfrak{q}\supseteq\mathfrak{p}
is very similar except this time
N:=H(R_{\mathfrak{q}}/\mathfrak{p}R_{\mathfrak{q}})
and we use that
\left(\nicefrac{{xD=0}}{{B|x}}\right)\subseteq\left(\nicefrac{{xc=0}}{{x=0}}\right)\cup\left(\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{a|x}}\right)
.
∎

Thus, for
R
an arithmetical ring, if there exists an algorithm deciding inclusions of finite unions of Ziegler basic open sets then
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
is recursive. Combining this with
[
GLPT18
, 7.1]
(and its proof) we get the following theorem.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain. There is an algorithm deciding inclusions of finite unions of Ziegler basic open sets if and only if
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
is recursive.
Moreover, if
R/\mathfrak{m}
is infinite for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\lhd R
then
T_{R}
is decidable if and only if
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
is recursive.

We finish off the work in
[
GLPT18
]
by showing that for
R
a Prüfer domain, if
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive then
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
n
(equivalently
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
is recursive).

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
R
be a recursive arithmetical ring. If
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive then
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
n
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{2}
and
a,c,b_{1},\ldots,b_{n},d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\in R
. Suppose that
\alpha,\beta,r_{1},r_{2},\allowbreak s_{1},\allowbreak s_{2}\in R
are such that

b_{1}\alpha=b_{2}r_{2},\ \ \ \ \ \ b_{2}(\alpha-1)=b_{1}r_{1}

d_{1}\beta=d_{2}s_{2}\ \text{ and }\ d_{2}(\beta-1)=d_{1}s_{1}.

Claim:

(a,c,b_{1},\raisebox{-3.0pt}{$\cdot$},b_{n},d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\text{DPR}_{n}(R)

if and only if

(1)

(a\alpha,c\beta,b_{2},\ldots,b_{n},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n})\in\text{DPR}_{n-1}(R)
,

(2)

(a\alpha,c(\beta-1),b_{2},\ldots,b_{n},d_{1},d_{3},\ldots,d_{n})\in\text{DPR}_{n-1}(R)
,

(3)

(a(\alpha-1),c\beta,b_{1},b_{3}\ldots,b_{n},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n})\in\text{DPR}_{n-1}(R)
and

(4)

(a(\alpha-1),c(\beta-1),b_{1},b_{3},\ldots,b_{n},d_{1},d_{3},\ldots,d_{n})\in\text{DPR}_{n-1}(R)
.

This claim plus the fact that we can always find appropriate
\alpha,\beta,r_{1},r_{2},s_{1},s_{2}\in R
implies the proposition.

To prove the forward direction, we show that
(\star)
implies
(i)
and note that the remaining conditions
(ii)
,
(iii)
,
(iv)
are the same as
(i)
but with the roles of
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
, and of
\alpha
and
\alpha-1
interchanged (respectively of
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, and of
\beta
and
\beta-1
interchanged).

Let
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
be prime ideals such that
\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q}\neq R
. Assuming
(\star)
, we need to show that
a\alpha\in\mathfrak{p}
,
c\beta\in\mathfrak{q}
,
b_{i}\notin\mathfrak{p}
for some
2\leq i\leq n
or
d_{i}\notin\mathfrak{q}
for some
2\leq i\leq n
.

Now
(\star)
implies
a\in\mathfrak{p}
,
c\in\mathfrak{q}
,
b_{i}\notin\mathfrak{p}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
or
d_{i}\notin\mathfrak{q}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. So the only problem is when
b_{1}\notin\mathfrak{p}
or
d_{1}\notin\mathfrak{q}
. Suppose
b_{1}\notin\mathfrak{p}
. If
\alpha\in\mathfrak{p}
then
a\alpha\in\mathfrak{p}
as required. So suppose further that
\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}
. Then
b_{1}\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}
and hence
b_{2}r_{2}\notin\mathfrak{p}
. So
b_{2}\notin\mathfrak{p}
as required. The argument when
d_{1}\notin\mathfrak{q}
is the same with the roles of
b_{1}
and
d_{1}
, of
b_{2}
and
d_{2}
, and of
\alpha
and
\beta
interchanged.

We now show that if
(\star)
is not true then one of
(i),(ii),(iii),(iv)
is not true. If
(\star)
is not true then there exist prime ideals
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
such that
\mathfrak{p}+\mathfrak{q}\neq R
and
a\notin\mathfrak{p}
,
c\notin\mathfrak{q}
,
b_{i}\in\mathfrak{p}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
d_{i}\in\mathfrak{q}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. For any proper ideal
I
, either
\alpha\notin I
or
\alpha-1\notin I
(respectively
\beta\notin I
or
\beta-1\notin I
). Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}
and
\beta\notin\mathfrak{q}
. Hence
a\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}
,
c\beta\notin\mathfrak{q}
,
b_{i}\in\mathfrak{p}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
d_{i}\in\mathfrak{q}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. So
(a\alpha,c\beta,b_{2},\ldots,b_{n},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n})\notin\text{DPR}_{n-1}(R)
as required.
∎

Combining this with the results in
[
GLPT18
]
we get the following.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain. There is an algorithm answering whether one Ziegler basic open set is contained in a finite union of others if and only if the relation
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive.

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain. The relation
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive if and only if there is an algorithm which, given a sentence

{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|\geq E_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}\left|\nicefrac{{\sigma_{j}}}{{\tau_{j}}}\right|=1,

where
\varphi_{i},\psi_{i},\sigma_{j},\tau_{j}
are pp-
1
-formulae and
E_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
, answers whether there exists an
R
-module
M
with
M\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
.
Moreover, if
R/\mathfrak{m}
is infinite for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\lhd R
then
T_{R}
is decidable if and only if
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
is recursive.

Proof.

The first statement follows from
3.8
and
2.5
.

For any
R
-module
M
and pp-pair
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}
,
\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}(M)
is an
R
-module. By
[
GLT19
, 3.1]
, if
R/\mathfrak{m}
is infinite for every maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\lhd R
then the only finite
R
-module is the zero module. So, the second statement follows from the first by a standard argument using the Baur-Monk theorem.
∎

Question 1
.

It was shown in
[
Gre15
, 3.2]
that, for any commutative ring
R
, if
T_{R}
is recursive then the radical relation is recursive. For
R
an arbitrary commutative ring, does
T_{R}
decidable imply
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
, or more generally
\mathrm{DPR}_{*}(R)
, is recursive?

3.2.
The sets
\mathrm{PP}(R)
and
\mathrm{EPP}(R)

In
[
GLT19
]
, a family of relations
\mathrm{PP}_{n}(R)
were introduced. It is shown,
[
GLT19
, 3.2]
, that if a recursive Prüfer domain has decidable theory of modules then
\mathrm{PP}_{n}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
n
. Conversely, it was shown that if
R
is a recursive
[4]
[4]

[4]

It was stated there for effectively given Prüfer domains. However, recall, if
R
is a recursive Prüfer domain with
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
recursive then
R
is effectively given.
Prüfer domain such that the value group of each localisation of
R
at a maximal ideal is dense then if
\mathrm{DPR}_{n}(R)
and
\mathrm{PP}_{n}(R)
are recursive uniformly in
n
then the theory of
R
-modules is recursive.

Definition 3.10
.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
consist of the tuples
(p,n,c_{1},\ldots,c_{l},d)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}\times R^{l+1}
such that there exist positive integers
s,k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}
and maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{m}_{s}
of
R
for which
n\in\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}\}
and for
1\leq i\leq s

(1)

\left|R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}\right|=p^{k_{i}}
,

(2)

c_{j}\in\mathfrak{m}_{i}
for
1\leq j\leq l
,

(3)

d\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
.

It is clear that for
R
a Bézout domain, if
\mathrm{PP}_{1}(R)
is recursive then
\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
l
. This is because
(p,n,c_{1},\ldots,c_{l},d)\in\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
if and only if
(p,n,\gcd\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{l}\},d)\in\mathrm{PP}_{1}(R)
. However, with a bit more work one can show this is also true for Prüfer domains.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain. If
\mathrm{PP}_{1}(R)
is recursive then
\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
l
.

Proof.

We skip this proof as it is very similar to the proof of
3.17
.
∎

As a direct consequence of
[
GLT19
, 6.1]
,
[
GLT19
, 3.2]
,
[
GLPT18
, 6.4]
,
3.11
and
3.7
, we get the following theorem.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain such that for all maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
, the value group of
R_{\mathfrak{m}}
is dense. The theory of
R
-modules is decidable if and only if
\mathrm{DPR}(R)
and
\mathrm{PP}(R)
are recursive.

We generalise
\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
to
\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
in order to deal with Prüfer domains with maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
such that
R_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a valuation domain with non-dense value group.

Definition 3.13
.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
consist of tuples

(p,n;a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma;e,m)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}\times R^{l}\times R\times R\times\mathbb{N}_{0}

such that there exist
h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and, for
1\leq i\leq h
, prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\lhd R
and ideals
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
such that
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{p}_{i}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\in I_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq h
,
|\oplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}|=p^{n}
and
|\oplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}|=p^{m}
.

We say the sequence
(\mathfrak{p}_{j},I_{j})_{1\leq j\leq h}
witnesses
(p,n;a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
. By convention,
(p,0;a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma;e,0)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
and the empty sequence is a witness for it. We will often write
\mathrm{EPP}(R)
for
\mathrm{EPP}_{1}(R)
.

Remark 3.14
.

We may replace prime ideals with maximal ideals in
3.13
without changing the definition since if
\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}}\right|
is finite then either
\mathfrak{p}
is maximal or
R_{\mathfrak{p}}=eR_{\mathfrak{p}}
and if
\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}}/I\right|
is finite then either
\mathfrak{p}
is maximal or
I=R_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

The relation
\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
is an extension of the relation
\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
p\in\mathbb{P}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n},d\in R
. Then
(p,n,c_{1},\ldots,c_{l},d)\in\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
if and only if
(p,n;c_{1},\ldots,c_{l};d;1,0)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
.

Proof.

Suppose
(p,n,c_{1},\ldots,c_{l},d)\in\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
. Let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
k_{1},\ldots,k_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{m}_{s}
be as in the definition of
\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
. Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{s}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be such that
n=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}k_{i}
. Then
c_{j}\in\mathfrak{m}_{i}
for
1\leq j\leq l
,
d\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
,
|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}(R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/\mathfrak{m}_{i}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}})^{\lambda_{i}}|=p^{n}
and
|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}(R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/1\cdot R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}})^{\lambda_{i}}|=p^{0}
. So
(p,n;c_{1},\ldots,c_{l};d;1,0)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
.

Suppose
(\mathfrak{m}_{j},I_{j})_{1\leq j\leq s}
witnesses
(p,n;c_{1},\ldots,c_{l};d;1,0)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
. For
1\leq i\leq s
, let
\lambda_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be such that
|R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/I_{i}|=|R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}|^{\lambda_{i}}
. If
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
then
I_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}_{i}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
and so
c_{j}\in\mathfrak{m}_{i}
for
1\leq j\leq l
. For
1\leq i\leq s
, let
k_{i}
be such that
|R/\mathfrak{m}_{i}|=p^{k_{i}}
. Now
n=\sum_{i=1,\ \lambda_{i}\neq 0}^{s}\lambda_{i}k_{i}
and hence
(p,n,c_{1},\ldots,c_{l},d)\in\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
.
∎

If the value group of
R_{\mathfrak{m}}
is dense then, for all
e\in R
,
\left|R_{\mathfrak{m}}/eR_{\mathfrak{m}}\right|
is either
1
or infinite. Moreover,
\left|R_{\mathfrak{m}}/eR_{\mathfrak{m}}\right|=1
if and only if
e\notin\mathfrak{m}
.

Remark 3.16
.

Let
R
be a Prüfer domain such that the value group of
R_{\mathfrak{m}}
is dense for all maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
. Then
(p,n;a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
if and only if
(p,n,a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},\gamma\cdot e)\in\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
and
m=0
.

In particular, if
R
is a recursive Prüfer domain such that the value group of
R_{\mathfrak{m}}
is dense for all maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}
then
\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
is recursive if and only if
\mathrm{PP}_{l}(R)
is recursive.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
R
be a recursive Prüfer domain. If
\mathrm{EPP}_{1}(R)
is recursive then
\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
l
.

Proof.

We show that for all
p\in\mathbb{P}
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},\gamma,e\in R
, if
\alpha,r,s\in R
are such that
a_{1}\alpha=a_{2}r
and
a_{2}(\alpha-1)=a_{1}s
then
(p,n;a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
if and only if there exist
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
,
m_{1}+m_{2}=m
,
(p,n_{1};a_{2},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma\alpha;e,m_{1})\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
and
(p,n_{2},a_{1},a_{3},\ldots,a_{l},\gamma(\alpha-1);e,m_{2})\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
. This is enough since we can always effectively find appropriate
\alpha,r,s\in R
.

Suppose that
(\mathfrak{p}_{j},I_{j})_{1\leq j\leq s}
witnesses
(p,n;a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
. For all
1\leq j\leq s
, either
\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}_{j}
or
\alpha-1\notin\mathfrak{p}_{j}
. By reordering, we may assume that
\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq t
and
\alpha-1\notin\mathfrak{p}_{j}
for
t+1\leq j\leq s
. Let
n_{1}=\log_{p}\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{t}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}\right|
,
n_{2}=\log_{p}\left|\oplus_{i=t+1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}\right|
,
m_{1}=\log_{p}\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{t}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|
and
m_{2}=\log_{p}\left|\oplus_{i=t+1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|
. Now
\alpha\gamma\notin\mathfrak{p}_{j}
and
a_{2},\ldots,a_{l}\in I_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq t
,
\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{t}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}\right|=p^{n_{1}}
and
\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{t}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|=p^{m_{1}}
. So
(p,n_{1};a_{2},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma\alpha;e,m_{1})\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
. Similarly,
(p,n_{2},a_{1},a_{3},\ldots,a_{l},\allowbreak\gamma(\alpha-1);e,m_{2})\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
.

Conversely, suppose that
n_{1},n_{2},m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are such that
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
,
m_{1}+m_{2}=m
,
(p,n_{1};a_{2},\ldots,a_{l};\allowbreak\gamma\alpha;\allowbreak e,m_{1})\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
and
(p,n_{2},a_{1},a_{3},\ldots,a_{l},\allowbreak\gamma(\alpha-1);e,m_{2})\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
. Let
(\mathfrak{p}_{j},I_{j})_{1\leq j\leq t}
witness
(p,n_{1};a_{2},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma\alpha;e,m_{1})\allowbreak\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
and let
(\mathfrak{p}_{j},I_{j})_{t+1\leq j\leq s}
witness
(p,n_{2},a_{1},a_{3},\ldots,a_{l},\allowbreak\gamma(\alpha-1);e,m_{2})\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l-1}(R)
. Then

\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}\right|=\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{t}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}\right|\cdot\left|\oplus_{i=t+1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}\right|=p^{n_{1}}p^{n_{2}}=p^{n}

and

\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|=\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{t}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|\cdot\left|\oplus_{i=t+1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|=p^{m_{1}}p^{m_{2}}=p^{m}.

For
1\leq j\leq t
,
\gamma\alpha\notin I_{j}
and hence
\gamma\notin I_{j}
and
\alpha\notin I_{j}
. Since
a_{2}\in I_{j}
and
\alpha\notin\mathfrak{p}_{j}
,
a_{1}\alpha=a_{2}r\in I_{j}
implies
a_{1}\in I_{j}
. Similarly,
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{m}_{j}
and
a_{2}\in I_{j}
for
t+1\leq j\leq s
. Therefore
(\mathfrak{p}_{j},I_{j})_{1\leq j\leq s}
witnesses
(p,n;a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
.
∎

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
R
be a Prüfer domain. If
T_{R}
is decidable then
\mathrm{EPP}_{1}(R)
(and hence
\mathrm{EPP}_{l}(R)
is recursive uniformly in
l
).

Proof.

For
p\in\mathbb{P}
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a,\gamma,e\in R
, let

\Theta_{(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)}
be the
\mathcal{L}_{R}
-sentence

\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{x=0}}\right|=p^{2m+n}\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{e|x}}\right|=p^{m}\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{xe=0}}{{e|x}}\right|=1\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{\gamma|x}}\right|=1\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{e^{2}a|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=1.

We show that, for
m,n
not both zero,
(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{1}(R)
if and only if there is an
R
-module satisfying
\Theta_{(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)}
. Hence the lemma holds.

Suppose
(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{1}(R)
. By definition, there exist prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots\mathfrak{p}_{s}\lhd R
and ideals
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
such that
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{p}_{j}
and
a\in I_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq s
,
|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/I_{i}|=p^{n}
and
|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}|=p^{m}
. Let
M:=\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/e^{2}I_{i}
. Then
M
satisfies
\Theta_{(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)}
.

Conversely, suppose there exists an
R
-module
M
satisfying
\Theta_{(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)}
. Since
M
is finite and non-zero, there exist maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{m}_{s}\lhd R
and proper ideals
J_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
such that
\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/J_{i}\cong M
. Since
M
satisfies
\Theta_{(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)}
, for
1\leq i\leq s
,
(J_{i}:e)\subseteq eR_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}+J_{i}
.

By
2.18
, either
eR_{\mathfrak{m}}+J_{i}=R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
or
J_{i}\subseteq e^{2}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}+eJ_{i}
. So, either
e\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
,
J_{i}\subseteq e^{2}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
or
J_{i}\subseteq eJ_{i}
. So, for each
1\leq i\leq s
, there exists
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
with
J_{i}=e^{2}I_{i}
.

Since
|e^{2}a|x/x=0(R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/e^{2}I_{i})|=1
,
a\in I_{i}
and since
|x=x/\gamma|x(R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/e^{2}I_{i})|=1
,
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
. Therefore
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{m}_{s}
and
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
are such that
a\in I_{i}
and
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq s
, and
|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/I_{i}|=p^{n}
and
|\oplus_{i=1}^{s}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}|=p^{m}
. Hence
(p,n;a;\gamma;e,m)\in\mathrm{EPP}_{1}(R)
.
∎

The following corollary is a direct consequence of the proof of
3.18
. We will later see,
7.6
, that the converse also holds.

Corollary 3.19
.

If the theory of
R
-modules of size
n
is decidable uniformly in
n
then
\mathrm{EPP}_{1}(R)
is recursive.

3.3.
The set
X(R)

Definition 3.20
.

Let
X(R)
be the set of
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}\times(R\backslash\{0\})\times R^{3}
such that there exist integers
h\in\mathbb{N}
and prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{h}
such that
\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|=p^{n}
and for
1\leq i\leq h
,
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
, and,
there exists an ideal
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
such that
a\in I_{i}
and
\delta\notin(I_{i})^{\#}
.

It is often easier to check that
X(R)
is recursive in concrete rings using the following reformulation.

Remark 3.21
.

Let
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}\times(R\backslash\{0\})\times R^{3}
. Then
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)\in X(R)
if and only if there exist
1\leq h\leq n
and maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{m}_{h}
such that
\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}\right|=p^{n}
, and, for
1\leq i\leq h

(1)

\gamma\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
, and,

(2)

either
\delta\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
, or, there exists a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}_{i}
such that
a\in\mathfrak{q}_{i}
and
\delta\notin\mathfrak{q}_{i}
.

Proof.

Note that if
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
in the definition of
X(R)
is such that
\left|R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|=1
then we may drop
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
from the sequence of prime ideals witnessing
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)\in X(R)
. Therefore, we may assume each
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
is maximal and that
1\leq h\leq n
. Now, if
a\in I_{i}
and
\delta\notin(I_{i})^{\#}
then either
I_{i}=R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
and
\delta\notin\mathfrak{p}_{i}
, or,
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
is a proper ideal. If
I_{i}
is a proper ideal then
a\in I_{i}
implies
a\in(I_{i})^{\#}
.

Therefore, if
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)\in X(R)
then the conditions in the statement hold with,
\mathfrak{m}_{i}:=\mathfrak{p}_{i}
, and
\mathfrak{q}_{i}:=(I_{i})^{\#}
if
\delta\in\mathfrak{m}_{i}
. Conversely, if the conditions in the statement hold for
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)
then set
\mathfrak{p}_{i}:=\mathfrak{m}_{i}
and,
I_{i}:=R_{\mathfrak{m}_{i}}
if
\delta\notin\mathfrak{m}_{i}
and
I_{i}:=\mathfrak{q}_{i}
otherwise.
∎

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
R
be a Prüfer domain. If
T_{R}
is decidable then
X(R)
is recursive.

Proof.

Let
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}\times(R\backslash\{0\})\times R^{3}
. We show that
(p,n;e,\gamma,a,\delta)\in X(R)
if and only if there exists an
R
-modules satisfying

{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}:=\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{e|x}}\right|=p^{n}\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{xe=0}}{{e|x}}\right|=1\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{e^{2}a|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=1\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{\gamma|x}}\right|=1\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{x\delta=0}}{{x=0}}\right|=1.

First suppose that there exist
h\in\mathbb{N}
and prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{h}\lhd R
such that

\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|=p^{n}
and for
1\leq i\leq h
,
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{p}_{i}
, and,
there exists an ideal
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
such that
a\in I_{i}
and
\delta\notin(I_{i})^{\#}
. Then
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/e^{2}I_{i}\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
.

Conversely, suppose there exists an
R
-module satisfying
\chi
. Then,
2.11
, there exists a finite direct sum of modules
U_{i}
such that
\oplus_{i=1}^{h}U_{i}\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
and each
U_{i}
is the restriction to
R
of a uniserial module over
R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
for some prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\lhd R
. We may assume that
U_{i}/U_{i}e
is non-zero for each
U_{i}
, for otherwise the direct sum with
U_{i}
omitted also satisfies
\chi
. Since
U_{i}
is uniserial as an
R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
-module and
U_{i}/U_{i}e
is non-zero and finite,
U_{i}
is finitely generated over
R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
. Therefore
U_{i}\cong R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/J_{i}
for some ideal
J_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
. Since
|xe=0/e|x(U_{i})|=1
,
(J_{i}:e)\subseteq J_{i}+eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
. So, as in
3.18
, there exists
I_{i}\lhd R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
such that
J_{i}=e^{2}I_{i}
.

Now, since
\oplus_{i=1}^{h}U_{i}\models{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}
,
\left|\oplus_{i=1}^{h}R_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}/eR_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\right|=p^{n}
,
e^{2}a\in e^{2}I
and hence
a\in I
. Moreover, by
2.15
,
\gamma\notin\mathfrak{p}_{i}
and
\delta\notin I_{i}^{\#}
.
∎

4.
Formalisms

The formalisms introduced in this section will be used throughout the paper to allow us to make reductions in complexity
of certain sets of conditions later on.

4.1.
Sets of functions

Let
\Delta
be a set and
\mathcal{E}
a set of functions from
\Delta
to
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
such that if
f,g\in\mathcal{E}
then
f\cdot g\in\mathcal{E}
and such that the function which has constant value
1
is in
\mathcal{E}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
X,Y\subseteq\Delta
be finite sets and let
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and
g:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.

Define
\Omega_{f,g,n}
to be the set of all tuples of functions
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})
where
f_{i}:X\cup(Y\backslash Y_{i})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and
g_{i}:Y_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
are such that
Y_{i}\subseteq Y
and

•

\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in X
,

•

f_{i}(y)<g(y)
for all
y\in Y\backslash Y_{i}
,

•

g_{i}(y)=g(y)
for all
y\in Y_{i}
, and

•

for all
y\in Y
,

\left(\prod_{i\text{ with }y\in Y\backslash Y_{i}}f_{i}(y)\cdot\prod_{i\text{ with }y\in Y_{i}}g_{i}(y)\right)\geq g(y).

The most important instance of this set up in this paper is when
\Delta
is a set of pairs
\varphi/\psi
of pp-
1
-formulae over a ring
R
and
\mathcal{E}
is the set of
R
-modules
M
viewed as functions on
\Delta
by setting
M(\varphi/\psi):=|\varphi(M)/\psi(M)|
.

For
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
sets of functions from
\Delta
to
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, define
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{2}
to be the set of
f:\Delta\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
such that there exist
f_{1}\in\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
f_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}
such that
f=f_{1}\cdot f_{2}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
X,Y\subseteq\Delta
be finite sets and let
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and
g:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. There exists
h\in\mathcal{E}
such that
h(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in X
and
h(y)\geq g(y)
for all
y\in Y
if and only if for some
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in\Omega_{f,g,n}
there exist
h_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
such that
h_{i}(x)=f_{i}(x)
for all
x\in X\cup(Y\backslash Y_{i})
and
h_{i}(y)\geq g_{i}(y)
for all
y\in Y_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
h\in\mathcal{E}
be such that
h(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in X
and
h(y)\geq g(y)
for all
y\in Y
. Since
\mathcal{E}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{i}
, there exist
h_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
such that
\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)=h(x)
for all
x\in\Delta
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
Y_{i}:=\{y\ |\ h_{i}(y)\geq g(y)\}
, let
f_{i}(x)=h_{i}(x)
for all
x\in X\cup(Y\backslash Y_{i})
, and let
g_{i}(y)=g(y)
for all
y\in Y_{i}
. By definition
\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)=h(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in X
,
f_{i}(y)=h_{i}(y)<g(y)
for all
y\in Y\backslash Y_{i}
and
g_{i}(y)=g(y)
for all
y\in Y_{i}
. Now if
y\in Y\backslash Y_{i}
then
f_{i}(y)=h_{i}(y)
and if
y\in Y_{i}
then
g_{i}(y)=g(y)
. Therefore, for all
y\in Y
, either
y\in Y_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
and hence the
4
th condition holds or
y\notin Y_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and so
g(y)\leq h(y)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(y)=\prod_{i}f_{i}(y)
. So
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in\Omega_{f,g,n}
.

Conversely, suppose
h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}:\Delta\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
are such that there exists
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n},\allowbreak g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\in\Omega_{f,g,n}
with
h_{i}(x)=f_{i}(x)
for all
x\in X\cup(Y\backslash Y_{i})
and
h_{i}(y)\geq g_{i}(y)
for all
y\in Y_{i}
. Define
h:\Delta\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
by
h(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)
for all
x\in\Delta
. Then
h(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in X
and
h(y)\geq g(y)
for all
y\in Y
as required.
∎

Definition 4.2
.

Let
X,Y\subseteq\Delta
be finite sets and let
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and
g:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. Let
g:=\max\{g(y)\ |\ y\in Y\}
. Define
\Theta_{f,g}
to be the set of pairs of functions
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
such that
f^{\prime}:X\cup(Y\backslash Y^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
g^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
f^{\prime}(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in X
,
g^{\prime}(y)=g
for all
y\in Y^{\prime}
and
g(y)\leq f^{\prime}(y)<g
for all
y\in Y\backslash Y^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.3
.

A function
h\in\mathcal{E}
is such that
h(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in X
and
h(y)\geq g(y)
for all
y\in Y
if and only if there exists
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\Theta_{f,g}
such that
h(x)=f^{\prime}(x)
for all
x\in X\cup(Y\backslash Y^{\prime})
and
h(y)\geq g^{\prime}(y)
for all
y\in Y^{\prime}
.

4.2.
Lattices generated by conditions

Let
W
be an infinite set. Let
\mathbb{W}
be the free bounded distributive lattice generated by
W
[5]
[5]

[5]

See
[
Grä11
]
for the definition of a free distributive lattice and add a largest and smallest element.
. We use
\sqcup
for the supremum in this lattice and
\sqcap
for the infimum in this lattice. Any element of
\mathbb{W}
may be expressed as
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}
where
I
and
J_{i}
for
i\in I
are finite sets and
w_{ij}\in W
. Moreover, for
v_{k},w_{ij}\in W
,

\bigsqcap_{k\in K}v_{k}\leq\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}

if and only if there exists
i\in I
such that

\bigsqcap_{k\in K}v_{k}\leq\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}

if and only if there exists
i\in I
such that

\{v_{k}\ |\ k\in K\}\supseteq\{w_{ij}\ |\ j\in J_{i}\}.

We make the convention that the empty infimum is the largest element
\top
and the empty supremum is the least element
\bot
.

We call an expression of the form
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}
, where
w_{ij}\in W
,
irredundant
if for each
i\in I
,
w_{ij_{1}}=w_{ij_{2}}
implies
j_{i}=j_{2}
and the sets
w_{i}:=\{w_{ij}\ |\ j\in J_{i}\}
for
i\in I
are pairwise incomparable by inclusion. If
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}
and
\bigsqcup_{i\in I^{\prime}}\bigsqcap_{j\in J^{\prime}_{i}}w^{\prime}_{ij}
are in irredundant form then
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I^{\prime}}\bigsqcap_{j\in J^{\prime}_{i}}w^{\prime}_{ij}
if and only if there exist bijections
\sigma:I\rightarrow I^{\prime}
and
\sigma_{i}:J_{i}\rightarrow J^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}
for each
i\in I
such that
w_{ij}=w^{\prime}_{\sigma(i),\sigma_{i}(j)}
for all
i\in I
and
j\in I_{i}
.

Given a recursive presentation of
W
(i.e. a bijection with
\mathbb{N}
), this presentation of
W
gives rise to a recursive presentation of
\mathbb{W}
(i.e a presentation where the inclusion of
W
in
\mathbb{W}
is recursive and
\sqcup
and
\sqcap
are recursive functions).

For any
V\subseteq W
, define
\mathbb{V}
to be the filter generated by
V
in
\mathbb{W}
. Note for
w_{ij}\in W
,
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}\in\mathbb{V}
if and only if there exists
i\in I
such that
w_{ij}\in V
for all
j\in J_{i}
. So, in particular
\mathbb{V}
is prime filter. It follows that
V
is a recursive subset of
W
if and only if
\mathbb{V}
is a recursive subset of
\mathbb{W}
.

Suppose that
\text{clx}:W\rightarrow\alpha
where
\alpha
is a partially ordered set with the descending chain condition. Let
\underline{w}\in\mathbb{W}
and let
\underline{w}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I}\bigsqcap_{j\in J_{i}}w_{ij}
be in irredundant form. For
\beta\in\alpha
, we write
\mathrm{clx}\underline{w}\leq\beta
if
w_{ij}\leq\beta
for all
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
and
\mathrm{clx}\underline{w}<\beta
if
\mathrm{clx}w_{ij}<\beta
for all
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
. Note that if
\underline{w}
is a lattice combination of elements
w_{i}\in W
for
1\leq i\leq n
then
\mathrm{clx}w_{i}\leq\beta
(respectively
\mathrm{clx}w_{i}<\beta
) for
1\leq i\leq n
implies
\mathrm{clx}\underline{w}\leq\beta
(respectively
\mathrm{clx}\underline{w}<\beta
).

Remark 4.4
.

Let
W
be an infinite recursively presented set and
V\subseteq W
. Suppose that
\alpha
is an artinian recursive partially ordered set,
\mathrm{clx}:W\rightarrow\alpha
is recursive and
W_{0}\subseteq W
is recursive. Suppose that there is an algorithm which given
w\notin W_{0}
computes
\underline{w}\in\mathbb{W}
such that
\text{clx}\underline{w}<\text{clx}w
and such that
w\in V
if and only if
\underline{w}\in\mathbb{V}
. Then
V
is a recursive subset of
W
if and only if
V\cap W_{0}
is a recursive subset of
W
.

The precise choice of
W
and
V
varies throughout this article.

To illustrate how this setup is used, let

R
be a recursive ring. Let
W
be the set of
\mathcal{L}_{R}
-sentences

\bigwedge_{\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\in X}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right|=f(\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}})\wedge\bigwedge_{\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\in Y}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right|\geq g(\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}})

where
X,Y
are finite sets of pp-
1
-pairs,
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
and
g:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.
Let
V
be the set of
w\in W
such that there exists
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
with
M\models w
. Then, by
2.4
,
T_{R}
is decidable if and only if
V
is recursive. Working with
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{V}
, rather than
W
and
V
directly, allows us to talk about more than one module at a time. For instance, for
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in W
, the condition

w_{1}\sqcap\ldots\sqcap w_{n}\in\mathbb{V}

says that there exist
R
-modules
M_{i}\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
with
M_{i}\models w_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

5.
First syntactic reductions

Recall that, for a recursive ring
R
, in order to show that the theory of
R
-modules is decidable, it is enough to show that there is an algorithm which, given a sentence of the form

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{s}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|=F_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{j=1}^{t}|\nicefrac{{\sigma_{j}}}{{\tau_{j}}}|\geq G_{j},

where, for
1\leq i\leq s
and
1\leq j\leq t
,
\varphi_{i}
,
\psi_{i}
,
\sigma_{j}
and
\tau_{j}
are pp-
1
-formulae and
F_{i},G_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
,
answers whether there exists an
R
-module satisfying it.

In
[
GLT19
]
, it was shown that if
R
is a recursive Prüfer domain then it is enough to consider sentences where the pairs of pp-
1
-formulae in
(\star)
are all of the form
\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}
and
\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}
.
The proof of this statement relies on
2.6
,
[
PT15
, 2.2]
and
the fact, by
2.2
and
2.7
, that every
R
-module is elementary equivalent to a direct sum of pp-uniserial modules. This is also true for arithmetical rings, and so, although not stated in
[
GLT19
]
, the result, with the same proof, also holds for arithmetical rings.

Theorem 5.1
.

[
GLT19
, 4.1]

Let
R
be a recursive arithmetical ring. If there exists an algorithm which, given a sentence

{\raisebox{1.5pt}{$\chi$}}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|=G_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{i=m+1}^{n}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|\geq H_{i},

where
G_{i},H_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}
are pairs of pp-
1
-formulae of the form
\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}
and
\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, answers whether there exists
M\in\textnormal{Mod}\text{-}R
satisfying
\chi
, then
T_{R}
is decidable.

We call any conjunction of sentences of the form

\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|=1\ \ \ \text{ or }\ \ \ \left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}\right|=1

an
auxiliary sentence
.

Convention:
In the sequel, we will use the symbol
\square
as a variable denoting either
=
or
\geq
when talking about conjunctions of sentences like
\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right|\square N
. It will be useful for us to extend this notation so that
\square
can also be the symbol
\emptyset
, where
\square
being
\emptyset
indicates that
\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi}}{{\psi}}\right|\square N
is omitted from the conjunction. For instance, when
\square_{1}
is
\emptyset
, the sentence

\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|\square_{1}D\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}\right|\square_{2}E
stands for
\left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}\right|\square_{2}E
.

In this section we improve
[
GLT19
, 4.1]
to prove the following.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
R
be arithmetical ring. If there exists an algorithm which, given a sentence
\chi
of the form

\left|\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}\right|\square_{1}D\wedge\left|\nicefrac{{xb=0}}{{c|x}}\right|\square_{2}E\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|=G_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{i=m+1}^{n}\left|\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}\right|\geq H_{i}\wedge\Xi,

where
\square_{1},\square_{2}\in\{\geq,=,\emptyset\}
,
d,c,b\in R\backslash\{0\}
,
D,E,G_{i},H_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Xi
is an auxiliary sentence and
\nicefrac{{\varphi_{i}}}{{\psi_{i}}}
are pairs of pp-
1
-formulae of the form
\nicefrac{{x=x}}{{c^{\prime}|x}}
and
\nicefrac{{xb^{\prime}=0}}{{x=0}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, answers whether there exists an
R
-module satisfying
\chi
, then
T_{R}
is decidable.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
X,Y
be finite subsets of pp-pairs of the form
\nicefrac{{d|x}}{{x=0}}
or
